[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Well... it's a bit hard to talk about. But what finally made me say something is that... after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. Then I just act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. \n\nWhich is easy enough to fake, honestly, because I am always tidying that kitchen. We just put in these new dark granite countertops a few months ago, and let me tell you, they show absolutely every single crumb and water spot. I swear I spend half my evening just wiping them down with a microfiber cloth. I work as a dispensing optician, so I spend all day wiping smudges off people's lenses and adjusting frames, so you'd think I wouldn't mind a bit of polishing at home, but it's just endless. \n\nAnyway... I'm sorry, what was I saying? Right, the... the making myself sick. Yes, I've been doing that, and just pretending everything is perfectly fine. That's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight when you eat and then make yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes, terrified. Absolutely terrified. It's funny you ask that, because just the other day my daughter was over—she brought her new puppy, a little golden retriever mix, just the sweetest thing but oh my goodness, the shedding! I had to vacuum the entire downstairs twice after they left. Anyway, she and a woman from my church choir—we sing every Sunday, mostly traditional hymns, which I just prefer so much more than that modern contemporary stuff they try to push sometimes—they both actually told me I look too thin lately. \n\nBut I don't see it. I really don't. If my skirt feels even a tiny bit snug when I put it on for work, or if I step on the scale and it's up even a single pound, I just... I panic. It's like I feel as if I've somehow let myself become huge overnight. So, yes, the fear of gaining weight is... well, it's always there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Normal? I... I don't really know what normal is supposed to look like anymore, to be perfectly honest. When I look in the mirror, I just... well, mostly I just notice that the lighting in our master bathroom is terribly unforgiving. We put in those daylight LED bulbs a few weeks ago because my husband said he needed brighter light for shaving, but it just washes absolutely everything out. \n\nIt’s actually the exact same issue we have at the optical shop. People will try on these gorgeous, expensive designer frames—like a beautiful tortoiseshell Tom Ford or a sleek little Prada number—and they instantly hate how they look because the fluorescent lights overhead cast such dreadful shadows on their faces. I always have to coax my clients to step over to the natural light by the front window before they make a final decision. It makes a world of difference for your complexion, it really does. \n\nUm... I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, my weight in the mirror. Right. I just... I don't know if it's normal. I really couldn't tell you. I just look and see all the places that feel wrong.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as fat even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. Like I was saying a few minutes ago about my daughter and my friend from church telling me I look too thin... I just can't see what they see. \n\nSpeaking of my friend from church, Susan, she actually makes a lot of her own clothes. She was showing me this beautiful periwinkle silk she bought for a wrap dress just the other day. I told her that color would perfectly highlight her eyes—she has these very striking, pale blue eyes. Honestly, though, she wears these incredibly heavy, dark rectangular frames that just completely overpower her face. I keep telling her she needs to come into the shop so I can fit her for something rimless, or maybe a soft translucent acetate to complement her delicate features, but she just won't listen. People get so attached to their glasses, even when they do absolutely nothing for them. \n\nAnyway... I'm sorry, what were we saying? Oh, right, seeing myself as fat. Yes, even with them telling me I'm thin, I still panic if my skirt feels even a tiny bit snug or if the scale goes up a single pound. I instantly feel as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. It doesn't matter what anyone else says to me, that's just how I feel when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, if I have a bad evening... you know, if I lose control and eat things I shouldn't... the next day I definitely have to make up for it. I'll just completely skip breakfast and lunch. Or I'll force myself to do extra laps with my morning walking group. \n\nWe walk at the community park every Tuesday and Thursday, and it really is a lovely park. They just put in these beautiful new cedar benches near the duck pond. Though, I will say, the paved path is getting terribly uneven because of the old oak tree roots pushing up through the asphalt. Brenda—she's the retired school teacher who organizes our routes—actually tripped last week and completely scuffed her brand new New Balance sneakers. Oh, she was just devastated. They were this bright coral color, which, between you and me, I thought was a bit flashy for her skin tone, but she loved them. I always tell my patients at the shop, you really have to match your accessories to your undertones, whether it's your glasses frames or your shoes! It's just basic color theory, you know? Warm undertones need warm colors, cool needs cool. \n\nAnyway... I'm so sorry, I lost my train of thought again. Oh! Right, the food and the walking. Yes, so I'll push myself to do those extra laps with the group, or I'll starve myself until dinner. And it's not because I'm feeling particularly energetic or wanting to enjoy the fresh air, it's purely just... well, I'm just desperately trying to undo what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Is it different from how others see me? I... I really don't know for sure. I mean, perception is such a tricky thing, isn't it? It's entirely subjective. \n\nIt actually reminds me of when we fit a client for their very first pair of progressive lenses. The brain just doesn't know how to process the different viewing zones right away. People will come back into the shop swearing up and down that the floor is tilted or that the walls are bowing inward. Just last week, Mr. Henderson—he's a retired accountant, lovely man, comes in every two years like clockwork—he brought his new varifocals back saying they made his golf club look bent like a banana. He plays over at the municipal course, the one with that gorgeous new clubhouse they just renovated. They used this stunning slate gray tile in the lobby that I just adore. I actually asked the manager where they sourced it because I'd love to redo our mudroom. My husband thinks it would be too dark, but with the right wainscoting, I think it would be incredibly elegant. \n\nOh, goodness, I'm rambling again. I'm so sorry. What did you ask me? My body shape, right. Um... honestly, I don't know what the reality is anymore. I know what I see, and I know they say something different, but I really couldn't tell you who is actually right at this point. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you knew for certain that you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I think I would still be terrified. Like I was saying earlier, even with my daughter and that woman from church constantly telling me I look too thin, it doesn't change the fear at all. \n\nSpeaking of skirts, I have this beautiful tailored navy pencil skirt I wear to the shop, and I actually had to take it to the dry cleaners on Elm Street last week. Have you been in there since they changed management? The new owners are very sweet, but they completely remodeled the front area and put in this massive reclaimed wood counter. It’s very trendy, I suppose, but it completely blocks the natural light from their front window! Lighting is just so crucial, especially in any kind of retail or service space. In our optical shop, we actually had to spend a fortune to install special daylight-balanced LED bulbs just so people could see the true color of the frames they're trying on. You wouldn't believe how many people buy a Havana tortoiseshell frame thinking it's a solid dark brown, only to step outside and realize it has bright orange flecks in it! If you have cool undertones, that warm orange will just completely wash you out. It's a total disaster for your complexion. \n\nAnyway, I'm so sorry, I've completely lost the plot again. The fear of gaining weight... right. Yes, even if I knew for an absolute fact that my weight was healthy, I know I'd still have that panic. Like I said, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a single pound, I still panic as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. It's like my brain just overrides whatever the logical truth is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look, rather than an actual concern about health?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it really is about how I look. Health doesn't really cross my mind when I'm panicking over the scale. It's all about the shape, the numbers, how my clothes fit... \n\nOh, speaking of clothes fitting, I actually had the most frustrating experience at the department store last weekend. I was looking for a new cardigan—something lightweight for the shop, because the air conditioning vent blows right over my dispensing desk. Anyway, I was in the petite section, and the sizing has just become completely arbitrary these days, hasn't it? A size eight in one brand is a size twelve in another! It's impossible. And the sales associate—bless her heart, she was very young, probably still in college—she kept trying to bring me these oversized, chunky knit sweaters. I had to explain to her that when you have a petite frame, a chunky knit just swallows you whole. It's the same principle as choosing a frame for a small face. You can't put a heavy, thick acetate frame on someone with delicate features, it just dominates their entire face! You need a thin metal wire, maybe a semi-rimless design... \n\nOh dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my husband always tells me I take the scenic route when I tell a story. What were we talking about? Oh, right, health versus looks. Yes, it's definitely about how I think I look. If it were just about health, I don't think I'd be skipping meals or doing all those extra laps just because my skirt felt a tiny bit tight. It's definitely the fear of looking heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape, rather than your actual weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's... it's the shape of it all. The way things sit on me. The actual number on the scale matters, of course it does, but it's really about the mirror. \n\nMirrors are so deceiving, though, aren't they? We actually had to replace all the mirrors in the shop last year because the old ones had this slight distortion at the edges. It was driving me absolutely mad. A client would put on a beautiful, expensive pair of Silhouette rimless frames, and they'd look in the mirror and think their face looked lopsided! I finally called a glass specialist from out of town—he was this fascinating man who used to work on restoring antique mirrors for historic homes. He was telling me about how they used to make glass with mercury and tin back in the day, which is why old mirrors have that gorgeous, cloudy, romantic look to them. It's incredibly toxic, of course, the mercury, but the craftsmanship was just extraordinary. He actually invited me and my husband to an antique glass exhibition in the city, but we couldn't go because it was the same weekend as our granddaughter's dance recital. She was a little bumblebee, and oh, she was just precious. She kept tripping over her little tulle wings, but she just popped right back up...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry, I've wandered off completely, haven't I? I always do that. You were asking about my body shape versus the weight. Yes, it's definitely the shape. If I look in the mirror and think my thighs look wider, or my stomach isn't flat, that panic sets in immediately, regardless of what the scale says. It's always about how I look in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape is accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know. I mean, my daughter says it's not accurate. She tells me I'm crazy for thinking I look big, but when I look in the mirror, I just see all the flaws. \n\nBut then again, seeing things accurately is a tricky business, isn't it? I mean, in my line of work, accuracy is literally everything. If an astigmatism axis is off by even a few degrees on a prescription, the patient will feel dizzy and nauseous all day. We had a gentleman in just last Tuesday—Mr. Henderson, lovely man, used to be a structural engineer—and his previous doctor had written his prescription with a positive cylinder instead of a negative one. It's a simple transposition error, happens all the time if someone is rushing, but poor Mr. Henderson thought he was losing his mind! He said the floor looked like it was slanting upwards. I had to sit him down and explain the whole optical physics behind it. It's fascinating, really, how our brains interpret visual information. The eye just gathers the light, but the brain is what actually *sees*. It makes you wonder how much of what we all see every day is just our brains filling in the blanks or playing tricks on us. Like those optical illusions where the two lines are the exact same length, but one looks longer because of the arrows at the end... \n\nOh, goodness, listen to me. I'm rambling about astigmatism now. I'm so sorry, I just get so passionate about the optics of it all. You were asking if my perception of my body is accurate. I guess... I really don't know. I feel like what I see is real, but everyone else tells me I'm too thin, so maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me, like Mr. Henderson's glasses. I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, since last winter, it's been... well, winter is always when things start getting so complicated. It's our absolute busiest season at the shop, you see. Everyone suddenly realizes they have flex spending dollars expiring on December 31st, and they all rush in at the exact same time wanting new progressives or prescription sunglasses. The lab gets incredibly backed up! We had this one week last December where the delivery courier actually lost a whole tray of finished jobs in the snow. Just left them on the loading dock of the completely wrong building! I spent three entire days on the phone trying to track down Mrs. Gable's bifocals because she was leaving for a cruise to the Bahamas that Friday. Can you imagine going on a cruise and not being able to read the dinner menu or see the evening shows? I always tell my patients, you simply must have a backup pair, especially if you have a high prescription. You wouldn't drive a car without a spare tire, would you? But people just don't want to spend the extra money, even though we offer a lovely fifty percent discount on the second pair if you buy them on the same day... \n\nOh, heavens, I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry. My brain just runs away with me when I think about work. You were asking about... throwing up. Right. \n\nWell, like I was trying to say, since last winter, it's been pretty constant. There has hardly been a single week where I haven’t had at least one of those... private eating spells, I guess you'd call them. Just eating so much when no one is around. And then the panic sets in, and I try to make up for it. So I'll make myself throw up, or I'll just skip my meals entirely the next day, or I'll completely overdo it with my walking, just pushing myself for miles and miles until my feet ache. But yes, to answer your question, there's barely been a week since winter where it hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about my weight? No, I really don't think I would. The whole reason I do it is because of the weight. In fact, what finally made me say something and seek help is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up, and then I just come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. \n\nMy husband never even notices. He’s usually completely engrossed in his history documentaries in the living room. He absolutely loves those World War II specials on PBS. We actually went to Normandy for our 25th anniversary, which was incredibly moving, but the flight over was just a complete nightmare. I lost my reading glasses—my absolute favorite pair of tortoiseshell cat-eyes, beautiful handmade Italian acetate—somewhere in Charles de Gaulle airport. The baggage handlers were on strike, of course, so it was absolute chaos, and I had to navigate the train system without being able to read the small print on the transit maps! I ended up having to buy a cheap pair of over-the-counter readers from a French pharmacy, and let me tell you, the optical center on those mass-produced lenses is completely atrocious. They induce so much unwanted base-in prism, it's a miracle I didn't get a blinding migraine right there in front of the Eiffel Tower. You really do get what you pay for when it comes to visual acuity...\n\nOh, dear... I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my mind just completely wandered off to Paris. You asked if I'd still do it if the weight didn't matter. No, definitely not. It's entirely about the weight. But the sneaking around, going upstairs to throw up and pretending I was just cleaning the kitchen... the hiding is what finally made me realize I had to tell someone what was going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose so. Even without a mirror, I think the fear would still be there. It's not just about what I see, it's how I feel in my clothes, you know? Even though my daughter and this lovely woman from my church—Mrs. Higgins, she sings in the choir—have both told me I look too thin, I still panic if my skirt feels even a little bit snug or if the scale goes up by even a single pound. It's as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. \n\nIt's funny you mention mirrors, though, because the quality of mirrors these days is just shocking. Most people don't realize that a cheap mirror can actually distort your reflection quite a bit. It has to do with the thickness of the glass and how the silvering is applied to the back. We have these specialized, high-definition mirrors in the optical shop so patients can see exactly how a frame looks on their face, and the clarity is just breathtaking. But you go into a department store dressing room, and the mirrors are usually bowed or warped. It's like a funhouse! I remember trying on a dress at Macy's last spring—a lovely floral wrap dress for my niece's graduation—and the mirror was so poorly mounted that it made my legs look like they were completely different lengths. It's all about the plane of the reflection. If the glass isn't perfectly flat, the light waves scatter, and... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again. Talking about light waves and mirrors. I'm so sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, even without a mirror, I think I'd still be terrified of gaining weight. The panic just... it just happens, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

She tells me I'm crazy for thinking I look big, but when I look in the mirror, I just see all the flaws. \n\nBut then again, seeing things accurately is a tricky business, isn't it? I mean, in my line of work, accuracy is literally everything. If an astigmatism axis is off by even a few degrees on a prescription, the patient will feel dizzy and nauseous all day. We had a gentleman in just last Tuesday—Mr. Henderson, lovely man, used to be a structural engineer—and his previous doctor had written his prescription with a positive cylinder instead of a negative one. It's a simple transposition error, happens all the time if someone is rushing, but poor Mr. Henderson thought he was losing his mind! He said the floor looked like it was slanting upwards. I had to sit him down and explain the whole optical physics behind it. It's fascinating, really, how our brains interpret visual information. The eye just gathers the light, but the brain is what actually *sees*. It makes you wonder how much of what we all see every day is just our brains filling in the blanks or playing tricks on us. Like those optical illusions where the two lines are the exact same length, but one looks longer because of the arrows at the end... \n\nOh, goodness, listen to me. I'm rambling about astigmatism now. I'm so sorry, I just get so passionate about the optics of it all. You were asking if my perception of my body is accurate. I guess... I really don't know. I feel like what I see is real, but everyone else tells me I'm too thin, so maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me, like Mr. Henderson's glasses. I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, since last winter, it's been... well, winter is always when things start getting so complicated. It's our absolute busiest season at the shop, you see. Everyone suddenly realizes they have flex spending dollars expiring on December 31st, and they all rush in at the exact same time wanting new progressives or prescription sunglasses. The lab gets incredibly backed up! We had this one week last December where the delivery courier actually lost a whole tray of finished jobs in the snow. Just left them on the loading dock of the completely wrong building! I spent three entire days on the phone trying to track down Mrs. Gable's bifocals because she was leaving for a cruise to the Bahamas that Friday. Can you imagine going on a cruise and not being able to read the dinner menu or see the evening shows? I always tell my patients, you simply must have a backup pair, especially if you have a high prescription. You wouldn't drive a car without a spare tire, would you? But people just don't want to spend the extra money, even though we offer a lovely fifty percent discount on the second pair if you buy them on the same day... \n\nOh, heavens, I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry. My brain just runs away with me when I think about work. You were asking about... throwing up. Right. \n\nWell, like I was trying to say, since last winter, it's been pretty constant. There has hardly been a single week where I haven’t had at least one of those... private eating spells, I guess you'd call them. Just eating so much when no one is around. And then the panic sets in, and I try to make up for it. So I'll make myself throw up, or I'll just skip my meals entirely the next day, or I'll completely overdo it with my walking, just pushing myself for miles and miles until my feet ache. But yes, to answer your question, there's barely been a week since winter where it hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about my weight? No, I really don't think I would. The whole reason I do it is because of the weight. In fact, what finally made me say something and seek help is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up, and then I just come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. \n\nMy husband never even notices. He’s usually completely engrossed in his history documentaries in the living room. He absolutely loves those World War II specials on PBS. We actually went to Normandy for our 25th anniversary, which was incredibly moving, but the flight over was just a complete nightmare. I lost my reading glasses—my absolute favorite pair of tortoiseshell cat-eyes, beautiful handmade Italian acetate—somewhere in Charles de Gaulle airport. The baggage handlers were on strike, of course, so it was absolute chaos, and I had to navigate the train system without being able to read the small print on the transit maps! I ended up having to buy a cheap pair of over-the-counter readers from a French pharmacy, and let me tell you, the optical center on those mass-produced lenses is completely atrocious. They induce so much unwanted base-in prism, it's a miracle I didn't get a blinding migraine right there in front of the Eiffel Tower. You really do get what you pay for when it comes to visual acuity...\n\nOh, dear... I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my mind just completely wandered off to Paris. You asked if I'd still do it if the weight didn't matter. No, definitely not. It's entirely about the weight. But the sneaking around, going upstairs to throw up and pretending I was just cleaning the kitchen... the hiding is what finally made me realize I had to tell someone what was going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose so. Even without a mirror, I think the fear would still be there. It's not just about what I see, it's how I feel in my clothes, you know? Even though my daughter and this lovely woman from my church—Mrs. Higgins, she sings in the choir—have both told me I look too thin, I still panic if my skirt feels even a little bit snug or if the scale goes up by even a single pound. It's as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. \n\nIt's funny you mention mirrors, though, because the quality of mirrors these days is just shocking. Most people don't realize that a cheap mirror can actually distort your reflection quite a bit. It has to do with the thickness of the glass and how the silvering is applied to the back. We have these specialized, high-definition mirrors in the optical shop so patients can see exactly how a frame looks on their face, and the clarity is just breathtaking. But you go into a department store dressing room, and the mirrors are usually bowed or warped. It's like a funhouse! I remember trying on a dress at Macy's last spring—a lovely floral wrap dress for my niece's graduation—and the mirror was so poorly mounted that it made my legs look like they were completely different lengths. It's all about the plane of the reflection. If the glass isn't perfectly flat, the light waves scatter, and... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again. Talking about light waves and mirrors. I'm so sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, even without a mirror, I think I'd still be terrified of gaining weight. The panic just... it just happens, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? Oh, no, absolutely not. The only reason I do those things is because of the weight. Like, after one of those bad evenings, the next day I’ll skip breakfast and lunch or push myself to do extra laps with my walking group, not because I feel energetic, but because I’m trying to undo what I ate. 

Speaking of the walking group, you would not believe the sunglasses some of these women wear out there in the sun. I try not to be a snob about my profession, I really do, but Mary showed up wearing these awful drugstore polarized lenses last Tuesday. I told her, "Mary, you simply cannot buy cheap polarization!" The film is just glued onto the outside of the lens, and the moment it gets hot or you sweat on it—which, of course, we do while walking—it starts peeling and bubbling at the edges. It causes terrible distortion. You need a lens where the polarizing filter is actually sandwiched between the layers of polycarbonate or Trivex. Trivex is really the best material for active wear anyway. It was originally developed by the military for helicopter windshields, did you know that? It's incredibly impact-resistant but much lighter than standard plastic, so your frames won't constantly slide down your nose when you perspire...

Oh, for heaven's sake. I've gone completely off track again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. I just can't help myself when it comes to eyewear, I get so passionate about proper UV protection. But yes, to answer your question... the restricting. I only skip those meals and do all that grueling extra walking to make up for the food. If I didn't care about my weight, I wouldn't do any of it.

But what finally made me say something is that... after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. We just put in these new dark granite countertops a few months ago, and let me tell you, they show absolutely every single crumb and water spot. I swear I spend half my evening just wiping them down with a microfiber cloth. I work as a dispensing optician, so I spend all day wiping smudges off people's lenses and adjusting frames, so you'd think I wouldn't mind a bit of polishing at home, but it's just endless. \n\nAnyway... I'm sorry, what was I saying? Right, the... the making myself sick. Yes, I've been doing that, and just pretending everything is perfectly fine. That's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight when you eat and then make yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes, terrified. Absolutely terrified. It's funny you ask that, because just the other day my daughter was over—she brought her new puppy, a little golden retriever mix, just the sweetest thing but oh my goodness, the shedding! I had to vacuum the entire downstairs twice after they left. Anyway, she and a woman from my church choir—we sing every Sunday, mostly traditional hymns, which I just prefer so much more than that modern contemporary stuff they try to push sometimes—they both actually told me I look too thin lately. \n\nBut I don't see it. I really don't. If my skirt feels even a tiny bit snug when I put it on for work, or if I step on the scale and it's up even a single pound, I just... I panic. It's like I feel as if I've somehow let myself become huge overnight. So, yes, the fear of gaining weight is... well, it's always there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Normal? I... I don't really know what normal is supposed to look like anymore, to be perfectly honest. When I look in the mirror, I just... well, mostly I just notice that the lighting in our master bathroom is terribly unforgiving. We put in those daylight LED bulbs a few weeks ago because my husband said he needed brighter light for shaving, but it just washes absolutely everything out. \n\nIt’s actually the exact same issue we have at the optical shop. People will try on these gorgeous, expensive designer frames—like a beautiful tortoiseshell Tom Ford or a sleek little Prada number—and they instantly hate how they look because the fluorescent lights overhead cast such dreadful shadows on their faces. I always have to coax my clients to step over to the natural light by the front window before they make a final decision. It makes a world of difference for your complexion, it really does. \n\nUm... I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, my weight in the mirror. Right. I just... I don't know if it's normal. I really couldn't tell you. I just look and see all the places that feel wrong.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as fat even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. Like I was saying a few minutes ago about my daughter and my friend from church telling me I look too thin... I just can't see what they see. \n\nSpeaking of my friend from church, Susan, she actually makes a lot of her own clothes. She was showing me this beautiful periwinkle silk she bought for a wrap dress just the other day. I told her that color would perfectly highlight her eyes—she has these very striking, pale blue eyes. Honestly, though, she wears these incredibly heavy, dark rectangular frames that just completely overpower her face. I keep telling her she needs to come into the shop so I can fit her for something rimless, or maybe a soft translucent acetate to complement her delicate features, but she just won't listen. People get so attached to their glasses, even when they do absolutely nothing for them. \n\nAnyway... I'm sorry, what were we saying? Oh, right, seeing myself as fat. Yes, even with them telling me I'm thin, I still panic if my skirt feels even a tiny bit snug or if the scale goes up a single pound. I instantly feel as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. It doesn't matter what anyone else says to me, that's just how I feel when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, if I have a bad evening... you know, if I lose control and eat things I shouldn't... the next day I definitely have to make up for it. I'll just completely skip breakfast and lunch. Or I'll force myself to do extra laps with my morning walking group. \n\nWe walk at the community park every Tuesday and Thursday, and it really is a lovely park. They just put in these beautiful new cedar benches near the duck pond. Though, I will say, the paved path is getting terribly uneven because of the old oak tree roots pushing up through the asphalt. Brenda—she's the retired school teacher who organizes our routes—actually tripped last week and completely scuffed her brand new New Balance sneakers. Oh, she was just devastated. They were this bright coral color, which, between you and me, I thought was a bit flashy for her skin tone, but she loved them. I always tell my patients at the shop, you really have to match your accessories to your undertones, whether it's your glasses frames or your shoes! It's just basic color theory, you know? Warm undertones need warm colors, cool needs cool. \n\nAnyway... I'm so sorry, I lost my train of thought again. Oh! Right, the food and the walking. Yes, so I'll push myself to do those extra laps with the group, or I'll starve myself until dinner. And it's not because I'm feeling particularly energetic or wanting to enjoy the fresh air, it's purely just... well, I'm just desperately trying to undo what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Is it different from how others see me? I... I really don't know for sure. I mean, perception is such a tricky thing, isn't it? It's entirely subjective. \n\nIt actually reminds me of when we fit a client for their very first pair of progressive lenses. The brain just doesn't know how to process the different viewing zones right away. People will come back into the shop swearing up and down that the floor is tilted or that the walls are bowing inward. Just last week, Mr. Henderson—he's a retired accountant, lovely man, comes in every two years like clockwork—he brought his new varifocals back saying they made his golf club look bent like a banana. He plays over at the municipal course, the one with that gorgeous new clubhouse they just renovated. They used this stunning slate gray tile in the lobby that I just adore. I actually asked the manager where they sourced it because I'd love to redo our mudroom. My husband thinks it would be too dark, but with the right wainscoting, I think it would be incredibly elegant. \n\nOh, goodness, I'm rambling again. I'm so sorry. What did you ask me? My body shape, right. Um... honestly, I don't know what the reality is anymore. I know what I see, and I know they say something different, but I really couldn't tell you who is actually right at this point. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you knew for certain that you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I think I would still be terrified. Like I was saying earlier, even with my daughter and that woman from church constantly telling me I look too thin, it doesn't change the fear at all. \n\nSpeaking of skirts, I have this beautiful tailored navy pencil skirt I wear to the shop, and I actually had to take it to the dry cleaners on Elm Street last week. Have you been in there since they changed management? The new owners are very sweet, but they completely remodeled the front area and put in this massive reclaimed wood counter. It’s very trendy, I suppose, but it completely blocks the natural light from their front window! Lighting is just so crucial, especially in any kind of retail or service space. In our optical shop, we actually had to spend a fortune to install special daylight-balanced LED bulbs just so people could see the true color of the frames they're trying on. You wouldn't believe how many people buy a Havana tortoiseshell frame thinking it's a solid dark brown, only to step outside and realize it has bright orange flecks in it! If you have cool undertones, that warm orange will just completely wash you out. It's a total disaster for your complexion. \n\nAnyway, I'm so sorry, I've completely lost the plot again. The fear of gaining weight... right. Yes, even if I knew for an absolute fact that my weight was healthy, I know I'd still have that panic. Like I said, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a single pound, I still panic as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. It's like my brain just overrides whatever the logical truth is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look, rather than an actual concern about health?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it really is about how I look. Health doesn't really cross my mind when I'm panicking over the scale. It's all about the shape, the numbers, how my clothes fit... \n\nOh, speaking of clothes fitting, I actually had the most frustrating experience at the department store last weekend. I was looking for a new cardigan—something lightweight for the shop, because the air conditioning vent blows right over my dispensing desk. Anyway, I was in the petite section, and the sizing has just become completely arbitrary these days, hasn't it? A size eight in one brand is a size twelve in another! It's impossible. And the sales associate—bless her heart, she was very young, probably still in college—she kept trying to bring me these oversized, chunky knit sweaters. I had to explain to her that when you have a petite frame, a chunky knit just swallows you whole. It's the same principle as choosing a frame for a small face. You can't put a heavy, thick acetate frame on someone with delicate features, it just dominates their entire face! You need a thin metal wire, maybe a semi-rimless design... \n\nOh dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my husband always tells me I take the scenic route when I tell a story. What were we talking about? Oh, right, health versus looks. Yes, it's definitely about how I think I look. If it were just about health, I don't think I'd be skipping meals or doing all those extra laps just because my skirt felt a tiny bit tight. It's definitely the fear of looking heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape, rather than your actual weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's... it's the shape of it all. The way things sit on me. The actual number on the scale matters, of course it does, but it's really about the mirror. \n\nMirrors are so deceiving, though, aren't they? We actually had to replace all the mirrors in the shop last year because the old ones had this slight distortion at the edges. It was driving me absolutely mad. A client would put on a beautiful, expensive pair of Silhouette rimless frames, and they'd look in the mirror and think their face looked lopsided! I finally called a glass specialist from out of town—he was this fascinating man who used to work on restoring antique mirrors for historic homes. He was telling me about how they used to make glass with mercury and tin back in the day, which is why old mirrors have that gorgeous, cloudy, romantic look to them. It's incredibly toxic, of course, the mercury, but the craftsmanship was just extraordinary. He actually invited me and my husband to an antique glass exhibition in the city, but we couldn't go because it was the same weekend as our granddaughter's dance recital. She was a little bumblebee, and oh, she was just precious. She kept tripping over her little tulle wings, but she just popped right back up...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry, I've wandered off completely, haven't I? I always do that. You were asking about my body shape versus the weight. Yes, it's definitely the shape. If I look in the mirror and think my thighs look wider, or my stomach isn't flat, that panic sets in immediately, regardless of what the scale says. It's always about how I look in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape is accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know. I mean, my daughter says it's not accurate. She tells me I'm crazy for thinking I look big, but when I look in the mirror, I just see all the flaws. \n\nBut then again, seeing things accurately is a tricky business, isn't it? I mean, in my line of work, accuracy is literally everything. If an astigmatism axis is off by even a few degrees on a prescription, the patient will feel dizzy and nauseous all day. We had a gentleman in just last Tuesday—Mr. Henderson, lovely man, used to be a structural engineer—and his previous doctor had written his prescription with a positive cylinder instead of a negative one. It's a simple transposition error, happens all the time if someone is rushing, but poor Mr. Henderson thought he was losing his mind! He said the floor looked like it was slanting upwards. I had to sit him down and explain the whole optical physics behind it. It's fascinating, really, how our brains interpret visual information. The eye just gathers the light, but the brain is what actually *sees*. It makes you wonder how much of what we all see every day is just our brains filling in the blanks or playing tricks on us. Like those optical illusions where the two lines are the exact same length, but one looks longer because of the arrows at the end... \n\nOh, goodness, listen to me. I'm rambling about astigmatism now. I'm so sorry, I just get so passionate about the optics of it all. You were asking if my perception of my body is accurate. I guess... I really don't know. I feel like what I see is real, but everyone else tells me I'm too thin, so maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me, like Mr. Henderson's glasses. I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, since last winter, it's been... well, winter is always when things start getting so complicated. It's our absolute busiest season at the shop, you see. Everyone suddenly realizes they have flex spending dollars expiring on December 31st, and they all rush in at the exact same time wanting new progressives or prescription sunglasses. The lab gets incredibly backed up! We had this one week last December where the delivery courier actually lost a whole tray of finished jobs in the snow. Just left them on the loading dock of the completely wrong building! I spent three entire days on the phone trying to track down Mrs. Gable's bifocals because she was leaving for a cruise to the Bahamas that Friday. Can you imagine going on a cruise and not being able to read the dinner menu or see the evening shows? I always tell my patients, you simply must have a backup pair, especially if you have a high prescription. You wouldn't drive a car without a spare tire, would you? But people just don't want to spend the extra money, even though we offer a lovely fifty percent discount on the second pair if you buy them on the same day... \n\nOh, heavens, I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry. My brain just runs away with me when I think about work. You were asking about... throwing up. Right. \n\nWell, like I was trying to say, since last winter, it's been pretty constant. There has hardly been a single week where I haven’t had at least one of those... private eating spells, I guess you'd call them. Just eating so much when no one is around. And then the panic sets in, and I try to make up for it. So I'll make myself throw up, or I'll just skip my meals entirely the next day, or I'll completely overdo it with my walking, just pushing myself for miles and miles until my feet ache. But yes, to answer your question, there's barely been a week since winter where it hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about my weight? No, I really don't think I would. The whole reason I do it is because of the weight. In fact, what finally made me say something and seek help is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up, and then I just come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. \n\nMy husband never even notices. He’s usually completely engrossed in his history documentaries in the living room. He absolutely loves those World War II specials on PBS. We actually went to Normandy for our 25th anniversary, which was incredibly moving, but the flight over was just a complete nightmare. I lost my reading glasses—my absolute favorite pair of tortoiseshell cat-eyes, beautiful handmade Italian acetate—somewhere in Charles de Gaulle airport. The baggage handlers were on strike, of course, so it was absolute chaos, and I had to navigate the train system without being able to read the small print on the transit maps! I ended up having to buy a cheap pair of over-the-counter readers from a French pharmacy, and let me tell you, the optical center on those mass-produced lenses is completely atrocious. They induce so much unwanted base-in prism, it's a miracle I didn't get a blinding migraine right there in front of the Eiffel Tower. You really do get what you pay for when it comes to visual acuity...\n\nOh, dear... I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my mind just completely wandered off to Paris. You asked if I'd still do it if the weight didn't matter. No, definitely not. It's entirely about the weight. But the sneaking around, going upstairs to throw up and pretending I was just cleaning the kitchen... the hiding is what finally made me realize I had to tell someone what was going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose so. Even without a mirror, I think the fear would still be there. It's not just about what I see, it's how I feel in my clothes, you know? Even though my daughter and this lovely woman from my church—Mrs. Higgins, she sings in the choir—have both told me I look too thin, I still panic if my skirt feels even a little bit snug or if the scale goes up by even a single pound. It's as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. \n\nIt's funny you mention mirrors, though, because the quality of mirrors these days is just shocking. Most people don't realize that a cheap mirror can actually distort your reflection quite a bit. It has to do with the thickness of the glass and how the silvering is applied to the back. We have these specialized, high-definition mirrors in the optical shop so patients can see exactly how a frame looks on their face, and the clarity is just breathtaking. But you go into a department store dressing room, and the mirrors are usually bowed or warped. It's like a funhouse! I remember trying on a dress at Macy's last spring—a lovely floral wrap dress for my niece's graduation—and the mirror was so poorly mounted that it made my legs look like they were completely different lengths. It's all about the plane of the reflection. If the glass isn't perfectly flat, the light waves scatter, and... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again. Talking about light waves and mirrors. I'm so sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, even without a mirror, I think I'd still be terrified of gaining weight. The panic just... it just happens, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to restrict your food intake if you didn't have the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? Oh, no, absolutely not. The only reason I do those things is because of the weight. Like, after one of those bad evenings, the next day I’ll skip breakfast and lunch or push myself to do extra laps with my walking group, not because I feel energetic, but because I’m trying to undo what I ate. \n\nSpeaking of the walking group, you would not believe the sunglasses some of these women wear out there in the sun. I try not to be a snob about my profession, I really do, but Mary showed up wearing these awful drugstore polarized lenses last Tuesday. I told her, \"Mary, you simply cannot buy cheap polarization!\" The film is just glued onto the outside of the lens, and the moment it gets hot or you sweat on it—which, of course, we do while walking—it starts peeling and bubbling at the edges. It causes terrible distortion. You need a lens where the polarizing filter is actually sandwiched between the layers of polycarbonate or Trivex. Trivex is really the best material for active wear anyway. It was originally developed by the military for helicopter windshields, did you know that? It's incredibly impact-resistant but much lighter than standard plastic, so your frames won't constantly slide down your nose when you perspire...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I've gone completely off track again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. I just can't help myself when it comes to eyewear, I get so passionate about proper UV protection. But yes, to answer your question... the restricting. I only skip those meals and do all that grueling extra walking to make up for the food. If I didn't care about my weight, I wouldn't do any of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

The actual number on the scale matters, of course it does, but it's really about the mirror. \n\nMirrors are so deceiving, though, aren't they? We actually had to replace all the mirrors in the shop last year because the old ones had this slight distortion at the edges. It was driving me absolutely mad. A client would put on a beautiful, expensive pair of Silhouette rimless frames, and they'd look in the mirror and think their face looked lopsided! I finally called a glass specialist from out of town—he was this fascinating man who used to work on restoring antique mirrors for historic homes. He was telling me about how they used to make glass with mercury and tin back in the day, which is why old mirrors have that gorgeous, cloudy, romantic look to them. It's incredibly toxic, of course, the mercury, but the craftsmanship was just extraordinary. He actually invited me and my husband to an antique glass exhibition in the city, but we couldn't go because it was the same weekend as our granddaughter's dance recital. She was a little bumblebee, and oh, she was just precious. She kept tripping over her little tulle wings, but she just popped right back up...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry, I've wandered off completely, haven't I? I always do that. You were asking about my body shape versus the weight. Yes, it's definitely the shape. If I look in the mirror and think my thighs look wider, or my stomach isn't flat, that panic sets in immediately, regardless of what the scale says. It's always about how I look in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape is accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know. I mean, my daughter says it's not accurate. She tells me I'm crazy for thinking I look big, but when I look in the mirror, I just see all the flaws. \n\nBut then again, seeing things accurately is a tricky business, isn't it? I mean, in my line of work, accuracy is literally everything. If an astigmatism axis is off by even a few degrees on a prescription, the patient will feel dizzy and nauseous all day. We had a gentleman in just last Tuesday—Mr. Henderson, lovely man, used to be a structural engineer—and his previous doctor had written his prescription with a positive cylinder instead of a negative one. It's a simple transposition error, happens all the time if someone is rushing, but poor Mr. Henderson thought he was losing his mind! He said the floor looked like it was slanting upwards. I had to sit him down and explain the whole optical physics behind it. It's fascinating, really, how our brains interpret visual information. The eye just gathers the light, but the brain is what actually *sees*. It makes you wonder how much of what we all see every day is just our brains filling in the blanks or playing tricks on us. Like those optical illusions where the two lines are the exact same length, but one looks longer because of the arrows at the end... \n\nOh, goodness, listen to me. I'm rambling about astigmatism now. I'm so sorry, I just get so passionate about the optics of it all. You were asking if my perception of my body is accurate. I guess... I really don't know. I feel like what I see is real, but everyone else tells me I'm too thin, so maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me, like Mr. Henderson's glasses. I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, since last winter, it's been... well, winter is always when things start getting so complicated. It's our absolute busiest season at the shop, you see. Everyone suddenly realizes they have flex spending dollars expiring on December 31st, and they all rush in at the exact same time wanting new progressives or prescription sunglasses. The lab gets incredibly backed up! We had this one week last December where the delivery courier actually lost a whole tray of finished jobs in the snow. Just left them on the loading dock of the completely wrong building! I spent three entire days on the phone trying to track down Mrs. Gable's bifocals because she was leaving for a cruise to the Bahamas that Friday. Can you imagine going on a cruise and not being able to read the dinner menu or see the evening shows? I always tell my patients, you simply must have a backup pair, especially if you have a high prescription. You wouldn't drive a car without a spare tire, would you? But people just don't want to spend the extra money, even though we offer a lovely fifty percent discount on the second pair if you buy them on the same day... \n\nOh, heavens, I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry. My brain just runs away with me when I think about work. You were asking about... throwing up. Right. \n\nWell, like I was trying to say, since last winter, it's been pretty constant. There has hardly been a single week where I haven’t had at least one of those... private eating spells, I guess you'd call them. Just eating so much when no one is around. And then the panic sets in, and I try to make up for it. So I'll make myself throw up, or I'll just skip my meals entirely the next day, or I'll completely overdo it with my walking, just pushing myself for miles and miles until my feet ache. But yes, to answer your question, there's barely been a week since winter where it hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about my weight? No, I really don't think I would. The whole reason I do it is because of the weight. In fact, what finally made me say something and seek help is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up, and then I just come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. \n\nMy husband never even notices. He’s usually completely engrossed in his history documentaries in the living room. He absolutely loves those World War II specials on PBS. We actually went to Normandy for our 25th anniversary, which was incredibly moving, but the flight over was just a complete nightmare. I lost my reading glasses—my absolute favorite pair of tortoiseshell cat-eyes, beautiful handmade Italian acetate—somewhere in Charles de Gaulle airport. The baggage handlers were on strike, of course, so it was absolute chaos, and I had to navigate the train system without being able to read the small print on the transit maps! I ended up having to buy a cheap pair of over-the-counter readers from a French pharmacy, and let me tell you, the optical center on those mass-produced lenses is completely atrocious. They induce so much unwanted base-in prism, it's a miracle I didn't get a blinding migraine right there in front of the Eiffel Tower. You really do get what you pay for when it comes to visual acuity...\n\nOh, dear... I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my mind just completely wandered off to Paris. You asked if I'd still do it if the weight didn't matter. No, definitely not. It's entirely about the weight. But the sneaking around, going upstairs to throw up and pretending I was just cleaning the kitchen... the hiding is what finally made me realize I had to tell someone what was going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose so. Even without a mirror, I think the fear would still be there. It's not just about what I see, it's how I feel in my clothes, you know? Even though my daughter and this lovely woman from my church—Mrs. Higgins, she sings in the choir—have both told me I look too thin, I still panic if my skirt feels even a little bit snug or if the scale goes up by even a single pound. It's as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. \n\nIt's funny you mention mirrors, though, because the quality of mirrors these days is just shocking. Most people don't realize that a cheap mirror can actually distort your reflection quite a bit. It has to do with the thickness of the glass and how the silvering is applied to the back. We have these specialized, high-definition mirrors in the optical shop so patients can see exactly how a frame looks on their face, and the clarity is just breathtaking. But you go into a department store dressing room, and the mirrors are usually bowed or warped. It's like a funhouse! I remember trying on a dress at Macy's last spring—a lovely floral wrap dress for my niece's graduation—and the mirror was so poorly mounted that it made my legs look like they were completely different lengths. It's all about the plane of the reflection. If the glass isn't perfectly flat, the light waves scatter, and... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again. Talking about light waves and mirrors. I'm so sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, even without a mirror, I think I'd still be terrified of gaining weight. The panic just... it just happens, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to restrict your food intake if you didn't have the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? Oh, no, absolutely not. The only reason I do those things is because of the weight. Like, after one of those bad evenings, the next day I’ll skip breakfast and lunch or push myself to do extra laps with my walking group, not because I feel energetic, but because I’m trying to undo what I ate. \n\nSpeaking of the walking group, you would not believe the sunglasses some of these women wear out there in the sun. I try not to be a snob about my profession, I really do, but Mary showed up wearing these awful drugstore polarized lenses last Tuesday. I told her, \"Mary, you simply cannot buy cheap polarization!\" The film is just glued onto the outside of the lens, and the moment it gets hot or you sweat on it—which, of course, we do while walking—it starts peeling and bubbling at the edges. It causes terrible distortion. You need a lens where the polarizing filter is actually sandwiched between the layers of polycarbonate or Trivex. Trivex is really the best material for active wear anyway. It was originally developed by the military for helicopter windshields, did you know that? It's incredibly impact-resistant but much lighter than standard plastic, so your frames won't constantly slide down your nose when you perspire...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I've gone completely off track again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. I just can't help myself when it comes to eyewear, I get so passionate about proper UV protection. But yes, to answer your question... the restricting. I only skip those meals and do all that grueling extra walking to make up for the food. If I didn't care about my weight, I wouldn't do any of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If I didn't care about how I looked? No, I really wouldn't. The whole reason I... I mean, I've said this, haven't I? The throwing up is entirely because of the weight and how I look. It's the only reason I do it. Like I mentioned earlier, the thing that finally made me realize I needed to talk to someone was the hiding. I'd eat too much, go upstairs to make myself sick, and then come back down pretending I was just getting ready for bed or tidying up the kitchen. 

Speaking of the kitchen, we just had the most gorgeous pendant lights installed over the island. They're this beautiful brushed brass, but the real star is the Edison bulbs we chose. People love the look of those vintage filaments, but they don't realize how much yellow light they cast. It can really strain your eyes if you're trying to read a recipe or chop vegetables. I actually brought home a pair of glasses with a specialized blue-light blocking tint—not the ugly yellow ones you see online, but a very subtle, premium anti-reflective coating that reflects the high-energy visible light away from the eye. It's fascinating how different wavelengths of light interact with the cornea and the lens of the eye. You see, when you're looking at something up close under warm artificial light, your eyes have to work harder to focus because the longer wavelengths...
