People will come back into the shop swearing up and down that the floor is tilted or that the walls are bowing inward. Just last week, Mr. Henderson—he's a retired accountant, lovely man, comes in every two years like clockwork—he brought his new varifocals back saying they made his golf club look bent like a banana. He plays over at the municipal course, the one with that gorgeous new clubhouse they just renovated. They used this stunning slate gray tile in the lobby that I just adore. I actually asked the manager where they sourced it because I'd love to redo our mudroom. My husband thinks it would be too dark, but with the right wainscoting, I think it would be incredibly elegant. \n\nOh, goodness, I'm rambling again. I'm so sorry. What did you ask me? My body shape, right. Um... honestly, I don't know what the reality is anymore. I know what I see, and I know they say something different, but I really couldn't tell you who is actually right at this point. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you knew for certain that you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I think I would still be terrified. Like I was saying earlier, even with my daughter and that woman from church constantly telling me I look too thin, it doesn't change the fear at all. \n\nSpeaking of skirts, I have this beautiful tailored navy pencil skirt I wear to the shop, and I actually had to take it to the dry cleaners on Elm Street last week. Have you been in there since they changed management? The new owners are very sweet, but they completely remodeled the front area and put in this massive reclaimed wood counter. It’s very trendy, I suppose, but it completely blocks the natural light from their front window! Lighting is just so crucial, especially in any kind of retail or service space. In our optical shop, we actually had to spend a fortune to install special daylight-balanced LED bulbs just so people could see the true color of the frames they're trying on. You wouldn't believe how many people buy a Havana tortoiseshell frame thinking it's a solid dark brown, only to step outside and realize it has bright orange flecks in it! If you have cool undertones, that warm orange will just completely wash you out. It's a total disaster for your complexion. \n\nAnyway, I'm so sorry, I've completely lost the plot again. The fear of gaining weight... right. Yes, even if I knew for an absolute fact that my weight was healthy, I know I'd still have that panic. Like I said, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a single pound, I still panic as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. It's like my brain just overrides whatever the logical truth is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look, rather than an actual concern about health?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it really is about how I look. Health doesn't really cross my mind when I'm panicking over the scale. It's all about the shape, the numbers, how my clothes fit... \n\nOh, speaking of clothes fitting, I actually had the most frustrating experience at the department store last weekend. I was looking for a new cardigan—something lightweight for the shop, because the air conditioning vent blows right over my dispensing desk. Anyway, I was in the petite section, and the sizing has just become completely arbitrary these days, hasn't it? A size eight in one brand is a size twelve in another! It's impossible. And the sales associate—bless her heart, she was very young, probably still in college—she kept trying to bring me these oversized, chunky knit sweaters. I had to explain to her that when you have a petite frame, a chunky knit just swallows you whole. It's the same principle as choosing a frame for a small face. You can't put a heavy, thick acetate frame on someone with delicate features, it just dominates their entire face! You need a thin metal wire, maybe a semi-rimless design... \n\nOh dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my husband always tells me I take the scenic route when I tell a story. What were we talking about? Oh, right, health versus looks. Yes, it's definitely about how I think I look. If it were just about health, I don't think I'd be skipping meals or doing all those extra laps just because my skirt felt a tiny bit tight. It's definitely the fear of looking heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape, rather than your actual weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's... it's the shape of it all. The way things sit on me. The actual number on the scale matters, of course it does, but it's really about the mirror. \n\nMirrors are so deceiving, though, aren't they? We actually had to replace all the mirrors in the shop last year because the old ones had this slight distortion at the edges. It was driving me absolutely mad. A client would put on a beautiful, expensive pair of Silhouette rimless frames, and they'd look in the mirror and think their face looked lopsided! I finally called a glass specialist from out of town—he was this fascinating man who used to work on restoring antique mirrors for historic homes. He was telling me about how they used to make glass with mercury and tin back in the day, which is why old mirrors have that gorgeous, cloudy, romantic look to them. It's incredibly toxic, of course, the mercury, but the craftsmanship was just extraordinary. He actually invited me and my husband to an antique glass exhibition in the city, but we couldn't go because it was the same weekend as our granddaughter's dance recital. She was a little bumblebee, and oh, she was just precious. She kept tripping over her little tulle wings, but she just popped right back up...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry, I've wandered off completely, haven't I? I always do that. You were asking about my body shape versus the weight. Yes, it's definitely the shape. If I look in the mirror and think my thighs look wider, or my stomach isn't flat, that panic sets in immediately, regardless of what the scale says. It's always about how I look in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape is accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know. I mean, my daughter says it's not accurate. She tells me I'm crazy for thinking I look big, but when I look in the mirror, I just see all the flaws. \n\nBut then again, seeing things accurately is a tricky business, isn't it? I mean, in my line of work, accuracy is literally everything. If an astigmatism axis is off by even a few degrees on a prescription, the patient will feel dizzy and nauseous all day. We had a gentleman in just last Tuesday—Mr. Henderson, lovely man, used to be a structural engineer—and his previous doctor had written his prescription with a positive cylinder instead of a negative one. It's a simple transposition error, happens all the time if someone is rushing, but poor Mr. Henderson thought he was losing his mind! He said the floor looked like it was slanting upwards. I had to sit him down and explain the whole optical physics behind it. It's fascinating, really, how our brains interpret visual information. The eye just gathers the light, but the brain is what actually *sees*. It makes you wonder how much of what we all see every day is just our brains filling in the blanks or playing tricks on us. Like those optical illusions where the two lines are the exact same length, but one looks longer because of the arrows at the end... \n\nOh, goodness, listen to me. I'm rambling about astigmatism now. I'm so sorry, I just get so passionate about the optics of it all. You were asking if my perception of my body is accurate. I guess... I really don't know. I feel like what I see is real, but everyone else tells me I'm too thin, so maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me, like Mr. Henderson's glasses. I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, since last winter, it's been... well, winter is always when things start getting so complicated. It's our absolute busiest season at the shop, you see. Everyone suddenly realizes they have flex spending dollars expiring on December 31st, and they all rush in at the exact same time wanting new progressives or prescription sunglasses. The lab gets incredibly backed up! We had this one week last December where the delivery courier actually lost a whole tray of finished jobs in the snow. Just left them on the loading dock of the completely wrong building! I spent three entire days on the phone trying to track down Mrs. Gable's bifocals because she was leaving for a cruise to the Bahamas that Friday. Can you imagine going on a cruise and not being able to read the dinner menu or see the evening shows? I always tell my patients, you simply must have a backup pair, especially if you have a high prescription. You wouldn't drive a car without a spare tire, would you? But people just don't want to spend the extra money, even though we offer a lovely fifty percent discount on the second pair if you buy them on the same day... \n\nOh, heavens, I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry. My brain just runs away with me when I think about work. You were asking about... throwing up. Right. \n\nWell, like I was trying to say, since last winter, it's been pretty constant. There has hardly been a single week where I haven’t had at least one of those... private eating spells, I guess you'd call them. Just eating so much when no one is around. And then the panic sets in, and I try to make up for it. So I'll make myself throw up, or I'll just skip my meals entirely the next day, or I'll completely overdo it with my walking, just pushing myself for miles and miles until my feet ache. But yes, to answer your question, there's barely been a week since winter where it hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about my weight? No, I really don't think I would. The whole reason I do it is because of the weight. In fact, what finally made me say something and seek help is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up, and then I just come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. \n\nMy husband never even notices. He’s usually completely engrossed in his history documentaries in the living room. He absolutely loves those World War II specials on PBS. We actually went to Normandy for our 25th anniversary, which was incredibly moving, but the flight over was just a complete nightmare. I lost my reading glasses—my absolute favorite pair of tortoiseshell cat-eyes, beautiful handmade Italian acetate—somewhere in Charles de Gaulle airport. The baggage handlers were on strike, of course, so it was absolute chaos, and I had to navigate the train system without being able to read the small print on the transit maps! I ended up having to buy a cheap pair of over-the-counter readers from a French pharmacy, and let me tell you, the optical center on those mass-produced lenses is completely atrocious. They induce so much unwanted base-in prism, it's a miracle I didn't get a blinding migraine right there in front of the Eiffel Tower. You really do get what you pay for when it comes to visual acuity...\n\nOh, dear... I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my mind just completely wandered off to Paris. You asked if I'd still do it if the weight didn't matter. No, definitely not. It's entirely about the weight. But the sneaking around, going upstairs to throw up and pretending I was just cleaning the kitchen... the hiding is what finally made me realize I had to tell someone what was going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose so. Even without a mirror, I think the fear would still be there. It's not just about what I see, it's how I feel in my clothes, you know? Even though my daughter and this lovely woman from my church—Mrs. Higgins, she sings in the choir—have both told me I look too thin, I still panic if my skirt feels even a little bit snug or if the scale goes up by even a single pound. It's as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. \n\nIt's funny you mention mirrors, though, because the quality of mirrors these days is just shocking. Most people don't realize that a cheap mirror can actually distort your reflection quite a bit. It has to do with the thickness of the glass and how the silvering is applied to the back. We have these specialized, high-definition mirrors in the optical shop so patients can see exactly how a frame looks on their face, and the clarity is just breathtaking. But you go into a department store dressing room, and the mirrors are usually bowed or warped. It's like a funhouse! I remember trying on a dress at Macy's last spring—a lovely floral wrap dress for my niece's graduation—and the mirror was so poorly mounted that it made my legs look like they were completely different lengths. It's all about the plane of the reflection. If the glass isn't perfectly flat, the light waves scatter, and... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again. Talking about light waves and mirrors. I'm so sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, even without a mirror, I think I'd still be terrified of gaining weight. The panic just... it just happens, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to restrict your food intake if you didn't have the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? Oh, no, absolutely not. The only reason I do those things is because of the weight. Like, after one of those bad evenings, the next day I’ll skip breakfast and lunch or push myself to do extra laps with my walking group, not because I feel energetic, but because I’m trying to undo what I ate. \n\nSpeaking of the walking group, you would not believe the sunglasses some of these women wear out there in the sun. I try not to be a snob about my profession, I really do, but Mary showed up wearing these awful drugstore polarized lenses last Tuesday. I told her, \"Mary, you simply cannot buy cheap polarization!\" The film is just glued onto the outside of the lens, and the moment it gets hot or you sweat on it—which, of course, we do while walking—it starts peeling and bubbling at the edges. It causes terrible distortion. You need a lens where the polarizing filter is actually sandwiched between the layers of polycarbonate or Trivex. Trivex is really the best material for active wear anyway. It was originally developed by the military for helicopter windshields, did you know that? It's incredibly impact-resistant but much lighter than standard plastic, so your frames won't constantly slide down your nose when you perspire...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I've gone completely off track again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. I just can't help myself when it comes to eyewear, I get so passionate about proper UV protection. But yes, to answer your question... the restricting. I only skip those meals and do all that grueling extra walking to make up for the food. If I didn't care about my weight, I wouldn't do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still have the desire to throw up after eating if you didn't have any concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about how I looked? No, I really wouldn't. The whole reason I... I mean, I've said this, haven't I? The throwing up is entirely because of the weight and how I look. It's the only reason I do it. Like I mentioned earlier, the thing that finally made me realize I needed to talk to someone was the hiding. I'd eat too much, go upstairs to make myself sick, and then come back down pretending I was just getting ready for bed or tidying up the kitchen. \n\nSpeaking of the kitchen, we just had the most gorgeous pendant lights installed over the island. They're this beautiful brushed brass, but the real star is the Edison bulbs we chose. People love the look of those vintage filaments, but they don't realize how much yellow light they cast. It can really strain your eyes if you're trying to read a recipe or chop vegetables. I actually brought home a pair of glasses with a specialized blue-light blocking tint—not the ugly yellow ones you see online, but a very subtle, premium anti-reflective coating that reflects the high-energy visible light away from the eye. It's fascinating how different wavelengths of light interact with the cornea and the lens of the eye. You see, when you're looking at something up close under warm artificial light, your eyes have to work harder to focus because the longer wavelengths... \n\nOh, for pity's sake. I'm doing it again. I'm so sorry. I just... I get talking about optics and I completely lose my train of thought. But yes, to answer your question, if I didn't care about my appearance, I wouldn't be sneaking upstairs to throw up. It's all because of how I look.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I think... I think it's really the fear. The sheer panic of gaining weight. Because, like I said before, my daughter and Mrs. Higgins from church keep telling me I look too thin, but it just doesn't register that way in my head. If the scale goes up by even a single pound, or if my skirt feels just a tiny bit snug around the waist, my heart just drops. I feel this absolute terror, like I've somehow let myself become huge overnight, and I just have to fix it immediately. 

It's actually a lot like when a patient comes in convinced their prescription is wrong because the new glasses feel "off." Most of the time, it's not the prescription at all, it's the base curve of the lens. The base curve is the front curvature of the lens, and if the lab changes it from what the patient is used to wearing, it can make the world feel like it's swimming or bowing out at the edges. The brain is so used to processing the world through one specific curve that when you change it, it causes this visual panic. We call it "swim effect." I had a gentleman in just last week—lovely man, retired accountant—who was absolutely certain we'd ruined his bifocals, but I just had to explain to him that the index of refraction in the new high-index plastic required a flatter base curve to fit in his semi-rimless frame. You see, the higher the index, the denser the material, which means light bends... 

Oh, dear. I'm so sorry. I've done it again, haven't I? The base curves and the... right. Yes. The throwing up. It's the fear of gaining weight. Even when people tell me I'm thin, that panic just takes over, and I feel like I have to do it.

I told her, \"Mary, you simply cannot buy cheap polarization!\" The film is just glued onto the outside of the lens, and the moment it gets hot or you sweat on it—which, of course, we do while walking—it starts peeling and bubbling at the edges. It causes terrible distortion. You need a lens where the polarizing filter is actually sandwiched between the layers of polycarbonate or Trivex. Trivex is really the best material for active wear anyway. It was originally developed by the military for helicopter windshields, did you know that? It's incredibly impact-resistant but much lighter than standard plastic, so your frames won't constantly slide down your nose when you perspire...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I've gone completely off track again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. I just can't help myself when it comes to eyewear, I get so passionate about proper UV protection. But yes, to answer your question... the restricting. I only skip those meals and do all that grueling extra walking to make up for the food. If I didn't care about my weight, I wouldn't do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still have the desire to throw up after eating if you didn't have any concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about how I looked? No, I really wouldn't. The whole reason I... I mean, I've said this, haven't I? The throwing up is entirely because of the weight and how I look. It's the only reason I do it. Like I mentioned earlier, the thing that finally made me realize I needed to talk to someone was the hiding. I'd eat too much, go upstairs to make myself sick, and then come back down pretending I was just getting ready for bed or tidying up the kitchen. \n\nSpeaking of the kitchen, we just had the most gorgeous pendant lights installed over the island. They're this beautiful brushed brass, but the real star is the Edison bulbs we chose. People love the look of those vintage filaments, but they don't realize how much yellow light they cast. It can really strain your eyes if you're trying to read a recipe or chop vegetables. I actually brought home a pair of glasses with a specialized blue-light blocking tint—not the ugly yellow ones you see online, but a very subtle, premium anti-reflective coating that reflects the high-energy visible light away from the eye. It's fascinating how different wavelengths of light interact with the cornea and the lens of the eye. You see, when you're looking at something up close under warm artificial light, your eyes have to work harder to focus because the longer wavelengths... \n\nOh, for pity's sake. I'm doing it again. I'm so sorry. I just... I get talking about optics and I completely lose my train of thought. But yes, to answer your question, if I didn't care about my appearance, I wouldn't be sneaking upstairs to throw up. It's all because of how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior of making yourself throw up after eating is more because of the fear of gaining weight or because you see yourself as overweight even when others say you are not?\n\nPatient: Well, I think... I think it's really the fear. The sheer panic of gaining weight. Because, like I said before, my daughter and Mrs. Higgins from church keep telling me I look too thin, but it just doesn't register that way in my head. If the scale goes up by even a single pound, or if my skirt feels just a tiny bit snug around the waist, my heart just drops. I feel this absolute terror, like I've somehow let myself become huge overnight, and I just have to fix it immediately. \n\nIt's actually a lot like when a patient comes in convinced their prescription is wrong because the new glasses feel \"off.\" Most of the time, it's not the prescription at all, it's the base curve of the lens. The base curve is the front curvature of the lens, and if the lab changes it from what the patient is used to wearing, it can make the world feel like it's swimming or bowing out at the edges. The brain is so used to processing the world through one specific curve that when you change it, it causes this visual panic. We call it \"swim effect.\" I had a gentleman in just last week—lovely man, retired accountant—who was absolutely certain we'd ruined his bifocals, but I just had to explain to him that the index of refraction in the new high-index plastic required a flatter base curve to fit in his semi-rimless frame. You see, the higher the index, the denser the material, which means light bends... \n\nOh, dear. I'm so sorry. I've done it again, haven't I? The base curves and the... right. Yes. The throwing up. It's the fear of gaining weight. Even when people tell me I'm thin, that panic just takes over, and I feel like I have to do it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still see yourself as overweight if you couldn't see your reflection in a mirror?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. That's an interesting question... if I couldn't see my reflection. I don't know, honestly. I suppose I might still feel heavy, just from how my clothes fit or how my body feels when I move, but the mirror definitely makes it worse. 

It's funny you mention reflections, though, because dealing with reflections is basically my entire day at the shop. The amount of glare people put up with is just astounding to me. They'll buy these beautiful, expensive frames and then decline the anti-reflective coating to save a few dollars. But what they don't understand is that without AR coating, about eight percent of the light just bounces right off the surface of the lens instead of passing through to the eye. It creates these terrible ghost images, especially when driving at night. You'll see halos around streetlights and headlights. And if you're doing a lot of computer work, the glare from the screen bouncing off the front of your lenses can cause such severe asthenopia—that's eye strain. A good multi-layer AR coating uses destructive interference, which is this incredible principle of physics where the light waves actually cancel each other out, allowing almost 99 percent of the light to transmit through...

Oh, good heavens. I am so sorry. I'm rambling about coatings now. I just... I don't know why I keep doing this. But to your question about the mirror, I really don't know for sure. I think I'd probably still feel like I was too big, even without seeing it.

And the sales associate—bless her heart, she was very young, probably still in college—she kept trying to bring me these oversized, chunky knit sweaters. I had to explain to her that when you have a petite frame, a chunky knit just swallows you whole. It's the same principle as choosing a frame for a small face. You can't put a heavy, thick acetate frame on someone with delicate features, it just dominates their entire face! What were we talking about? Oh, right, health versus looks. Yes, it's definitely about how I think I look. If it were just about health, I don't think I'd be skipping meals or doing all those extra laps just because my skirt felt a tiny bit tight. It's definitely the fear of looking heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape, rather than your actual weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's... it's the shape of it all. The way things sit on me. The actual number on the scale matters, of course it does, but it's really about the mirror. \n\nMirrors are so deceiving, though, aren't they? We actually had to replace all the mirrors in the shop last year because the old ones had this slight distortion at the edges. It was driving me absolutely mad. A client would put on a beautiful, expensive pair of Silhouette rimless frames, and they'd look in the mirror and think their face looked lopsided! I finally called a glass specialist from out of town—he was this fascinating man who used to work on restoring antique mirrors for historic homes. He was telling me about how they used to make glass with mercury and tin back in the day, which is why old mirrors have that gorgeous, cloudy, romantic look to them. It's incredibly toxic, of course, the mercury, but the craftsmanship was just extraordinary. He actually invited me and my husband to an antique glass exhibition in the city, but we couldn't go because it was the same weekend as our granddaughter's dance recital. She was a little bumblebee, and oh, she was just precious. She kept tripping over her little tulle wings, but she just popped right back up...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry, I've wandered off completely, haven't I? I always do that. You were asking about my body shape versus the weight. Yes, it's definitely the shape. If I look in the mirror and think my thighs look wider, or my stomach isn't flat, that panic sets in immediately, regardless of what the scale says. It's always about how I look in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape is accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know. I mean, my daughter says it's not accurate. She tells me I'm crazy for thinking I look big, but when I look in the mirror, I just see all the flaws. \n\nBut then again, seeing things accurately is a tricky business, isn't it? I mean, in my line of work, accuracy is literally everything. If an astigmatism axis is off by even a few degrees on a prescription, the patient will feel dizzy and nauseous all day. We had a gentleman in just last Tuesday—Mr. Henderson, lovely man, used to be a structural engineer—and his previous doctor had written his prescription with a positive cylinder instead of a negative one. It's a simple transposition error, happens all the time if someone is rushing, but poor Mr. Henderson thought he was losing his mind! He said the floor looked like it was slanting upwards. I had to sit him down and explain the whole optical physics behind it. It's fascinating, really, how our brains interpret visual information. The eye just gathers the light, but the brain is what actually *sees*. It makes you wonder how much of what we all see every day is just our brains filling in the blanks or playing tricks on us. Like those optical illusions where the two lines are the exact same length, but one looks longer because of the arrows at the end... \n\nOh, goodness, listen to me. I'm rambling about astigmatism now. I'm so sorry, I just get so passionate about the optics of it all. You were asking if my perception of my body is accurate. I guess... I really don't know. I feel like what I see is real, but everyone else tells me I'm too thin, so maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me, like Mr. Henderson's glasses. I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, since last winter, it's been... well, winter is always when things start getting so complicated. It's our absolute busiest season at the shop, you see. Everyone suddenly realizes they have flex spending dollars expiring on December 31st, and they all rush in at the exact same time wanting new progressives or prescription sunglasses. The lab gets incredibly backed up! We had this one week last December where the delivery courier actually lost a whole tray of finished jobs in the snow. Just left them on the loading dock of the completely wrong building! I spent three entire days on the phone trying to track down Mrs. Gable's bifocals because she was leaving for a cruise to the Bahamas that Friday. Can you imagine going on a cruise and not being able to read the dinner menu or see the evening shows? I always tell my patients, you simply must have a backup pair, especially if you have a high prescription. You wouldn't drive a car without a spare tire, would you? The base curve is the front curvature of the lens, and if the lab changes it from what the patient is used to wearing, it can make the world feel like it's swimming or bowing out at the edges. The brain is so used to processing the world through one specific curve that when you change it, it causes this visual panic. It creates these terrible ghost images, especially when driving at night. You'll see halos around streetlights and headlights. And if you're doing a lot of computer work, the glare from the screen bouncing off the front of your lenses can cause such severe asthenopia—that's eye strain. A good multi-layer AR coating uses destructive interference, which is this incredible principle of physics where the light waves actually cancel each other out, allowing almost 99 percent of the light to transmit through...\n\nOh, good heavens. It's all about the plane of the reflection. If the glass isn't perfectly flat, the light waves scatter, and... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again. Talking about light waves and mirrors. I'm so sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, even without a mirror, I think I'd still be terrified of gaining weight. The panic just... it just happens, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to restrict your food intake if you didn't have the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? Oh, no, absolutely not. The only reason I do those things is because of the weight. Like, after one of those bad evenings, the next day I’ll skip breakfast and lunch or push myself to do extra laps with my walking group, not because I feel energetic, but because I’m trying to undo what I ate. \n\nSpeaking of the walking group, you would not believe the sunglasses some of these women wear out there in the sun. I try not to be a snob about my profession, I really do, but Mary showed up wearing these awful drugstore polarized lenses last Tuesday. I told her, \"Mary, you simply cannot buy cheap polarization!\" The film is just glued onto the outside of the lens, and the moment it gets hot or you sweat on it—which, of course, we do while walking—it starts peeling and bubbling at the edges. It causes terrible distortion. You need a lens where the polarizing filter is actually sandwiched between the layers of polycarbonate or Trivex. Trivex is really the best material for active wear anyway. It was originally developed by the military for helicopter windshields, did you know that? It's incredibly impact-resistant but much lighter than standard plastic, so your frames won't constantly slide down your nose when you perspire...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I've gone completely off track again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. I just can't help myself when it comes to eyewear, I get so passionate about proper UV protection. But yes, to answer your question... the restricting. I only skip those meals and do all that grueling extra walking to make up for the food. If I didn't care about my weight, I wouldn't do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still have the desire to throw up after eating if you didn't have any concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about how I looked? I'd eat too much, go upstairs to make myself sick, and then come back down pretending I was just getting ready for bed or tidying up the kitchen. \n\nSpeaking of the kitchen, we just had the most gorgeous pendant lights installed over the island. I'm so sorry. I just... I get talking about optics and I completely lose my train of thought. But yes, to answer your question, if I didn't care about my appearance, I wouldn't be sneaking upstairs to throw up. I think it's really the fear. The sheer panic of gaining weight. Because, like I said before, my daughter and Mrs. Higgins from church keep telling me I look too thin, but it just doesn't register that way in my head. If the scale goes up by even a single pound, or if my skirt feels just a tiny bit snug around the waist, my heart just drops. I feel this absolute terror, like I've somehow let myself become huge overnight, and I just have to fix it immediately. \n\nIt's actually a lot like when a patient comes in convinced their prescription is wrong because the new glasses feel \"off.\" Most of the time, it's not the prescription at all, it's the base curve of the lens. The base curve is the front curvature of the lens, and if the lab changes it from what the patient is used to wearing, it can make the world feel like it's swimming or bowing out at the edges. The brain is so used to processing the world through one specific curve that when you change it, it causes this visual panic. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If I had a more accurate perception... I honestly don't know. It's hard to even imagine what that would look like, to see myself the way other people supposedly do. Would it stop me from making myself sick? Maybe? I'm just really not sure. 

It's a bit like... well, speaking of accurate perception, it is absolutely fascinating how subjective human vision actually is. We fit people for progressive lenses all the time, and their expectation is always this perfect, seamless vision at every distance. But the reality of a progressive lens design is that there is always inherent distortion in the lower periphery—it's mathematically unavoidable when you're blending multiple prescriptions into one piece of plastic or glass. We call it surface astigmatism. The brain literally has to learn to ignore those blurry edges and train the eye to drop down into the central corridor of clear vision. Some patients adapt in a day, their brains just remap the visual field and they see perfectly. But others just cannot handle the peripheral distortion at all; they feel like the floor is tilted or they're walking in a fishbowl. It all comes down to neuroplasticity and how the visual cortex processes the optical data coming through the optic nerve...

Oh, Lord. I've gone right off the rails again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. My husband always says I could talk to a brick wall about lens designs. What were we... right, my body shape. Yes. I just... I really don't know if having an accurate perception would change the throwing up. I can't quite picture what seeing myself accurately would even feel like.

Yes, so I'll push myself to do those extra laps with the group, or I'll starve myself until dinner. And it's not because I'm feeling particularly energetic or wanting to enjoy the fresh air, it's purely just... well, I'm just desperately trying to undo what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the way you think it is, or is it different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Is it different from how others see me? I... I really don't know for sure. I mean, perception is such a tricky thing, isn't it? It's entirely subjective. \n\nIt actually reminds me of when we fit a client for their very first pair of progressive lenses. The brain just doesn't know how to process the different viewing zones right away. People will come back into the shop swearing up and down that the floor is tilted or that the walls are bowing inward. Just last week, Mr. Henderson—he's a retired accountant, lovely man, comes in every two years like clockwork—he brought his new varifocals back saying they made his golf club look bent like a banana. He plays over at the municipal course, the one with that gorgeous new clubhouse they just renovated. They used this stunning slate gray tile in the lobby that I just adore. I actually asked the manager where they sourced it because I'd love to redo our mudroom. My husband thinks it would be too dark, but with the right wainscoting, I think it would be incredibly elegant. \n\nOh, goodness, I'm rambling again. I'm so sorry. What did you ask me? My body shape, right. Um... honestly, I don't know what the reality is anymore. I know what I see, and I know they say something different, but I really couldn't tell you who is actually right at this point. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you knew for certain that you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I think I would still be terrified. Like I was saying earlier, even with my daughter and that woman from church constantly telling me I look too thin, it doesn't change the fear at all. \n\nSpeaking of skirts, I have this beautiful tailored navy pencil skirt I wear to the shop, and I actually had to take it to the dry cleaners on Elm Street last week. Have you been in there since they changed management? The new owners are very sweet, but they completely remodeled the front area and put in this massive reclaimed wood counter. It’s very trendy, I suppose, but it completely blocks the natural light from their front window! Lighting is just so crucial, especially in any kind of retail or service space. In our optical shop, we actually had to spend a fortune to install special daylight-balanced LED bulbs just so people could see the true color of the frames they're trying on. You wouldn't believe how many people buy a Havana tortoiseshell frame thinking it's a solid dark brown, only to step outside and realize it has bright orange flecks in it! If you have cool undertones, that warm orange will just completely wash you out. It's a total disaster for your complexion. \n\nAnyway, I'm so sorry, I've completely lost the plot again. The fear of gaining weight... right. Yes, even if I knew for an absolute fact that my weight was healthy, I know I'd still have that panic. Like I said, if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a single pound, I still panic as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. It's like my brain just overrides whatever the logical truth is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you look, rather than an actual concern about health?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it really is about how I look. Health doesn't really cross my mind when I'm panicking over the scale. It's all about the shape, the numbers, how my clothes fit... \n\nOh, speaking of clothes fitting, I actually had the most frustrating experience at the department store last weekend. I was looking for a new cardigan—something lightweight for the shop, because the air conditioning vent blows right over my dispensing desk. Anyway, I was in the petite section, and the sizing has just become completely arbitrary these days, hasn't it? A size eight in one brand is a size twelve in another! It's impossible. And the sales associate—bless her heart, she was very young, probably still in college—she kept trying to bring me these oversized, chunky knit sweaters. I had to explain to her that when you have a petite frame, a chunky knit just swallows you whole. It's the same principle as choosing a frame for a small face. You can't put a heavy, thick acetate frame on someone with delicate features, it just dominates their entire face! You need a thin metal wire, maybe a semi-rimless design... \n\nOh dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my husband always tells me I take the scenic route when I tell a story. What were we talking about? Oh, right, health versus looks. Yes, it's definitely about how I think I look. If it were just about health, I don't think I'd be skipping meals or doing all those extra laps just because my skirt felt a tiny bit tight. It's definitely the fear of looking heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you perceive your body shape, rather than your actual weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's... it's the shape of it all. The way things sit on me. The actual number on the scale matters, of course it does, but it's really about the mirror. \n\nMirrors are so deceiving, though, aren't they? We actually had to replace all the mirrors in the shop last year because the old ones had this slight distortion at the edges. It was driving me absolutely mad. A client would put on a beautiful, expensive pair of Silhouette rimless frames, and they'd look in the mirror and think their face looked lopsided! I finally called a glass specialist from out of town—he was this fascinating man who used to work on restoring antique mirrors for historic homes. He was telling me about how they used to make glass with mercury and tin back in the day, which is why old mirrors have that gorgeous, cloudy, romantic look to them. It's incredibly toxic, of course, the mercury, but the craftsmanship was just extraordinary. You were asking about my body shape versus the weight. Yes, it's definitely the shape. If I look in the mirror and think my thighs look wider, or my stomach isn't flat, that panic sets in immediately, regardless of what the scale says. It's always about how I look in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape is accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know. I mean, my daughter says it's not accurate. She tells me I'm crazy for thinking I look big, but when I look in the mirror, I just see all the flaws. \n\nBut then again, seeing things accurately is a tricky business, isn't it? I mean, in my line of work, accuracy is literally everything. If an astigmatism axis is off by even a few degrees on a prescription, the patient will feel dizzy and nauseous all day. We had a gentleman in just last Tuesday—Mr. Henderson, lovely man, used to be a structural engineer—and his previous doctor had written his prescription with a positive cylinder instead of a negative one. It's a simple transposition error, happens all the time if someone is rushing, but poor Mr. Henderson thought he was losing his mind! He said the floor looked like it was slanting upwards. I had to sit him down and explain the whole optical physics behind it. It's fascinating, really, how our brains interpret visual information. The eye just gathers the light, but the brain is what actually *sees*. It makes you wonder how much of what we all see every day is just our brains filling in the blanks or playing tricks on us. Like those optical illusions where the two lines are the exact same length, but one looks longer because of the arrows at the end... \n\nOh, goodness, listen to me. I'm rambling about astigmatism now. I'm so sorry, I just get so passionate about the optics of it all. You were asking if my perception of my body is accurate. I guess... I really don't know. I feel like what I see is real, but everyone else tells me I'm too thin, so maybe my brain is just playing tricks on me, like Mr. Henderson's glasses. I honestly couldn't tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Well, since last winter, it's been... well, winter is always when things start getting so complicated. It's our absolute busiest season at the shop, you see. Everyone suddenly realizes they have flex spending dollars expiring on December 31st, and they all rush in at the exact same time wanting new progressives or prescription sunglasses. The lab gets incredibly backed up! We had this one week last December where the delivery courier actually lost a whole tray of finished jobs in the snow. Just left them on the loading dock of the completely wrong building! I spent three entire days on the phone trying to track down Mrs. Gable's bifocals because she was leaving for a cruise to the Bahamas that Friday. Can you imagine going on a cruise and not being able to read the dinner menu or see the evening shows? I always tell my patients, you simply must have a backup pair, especially if you have a high prescription. You wouldn't drive a car without a spare tire, would you? But people just don't want to spend the extra money, even though we offer a lovely fifty percent discount on the second pair if you buy them on the same day... \n\nOh, heavens, I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? I am so sorry. My brain just runs away with me when I think about work. You were asking about... throwing up. Right. \n\nWell, like I was trying to say, since last winter, it's been pretty constant. There has hardly been a single week where I haven’t had at least one of those... private eating spells, I guess you'd call them. Just eating so much when no one is around. And then the panic sets in, and I try to make up for it. So I'll make myself throw up, or I'll just skip my meals entirely the next day, or I'll completely overdo it with my walking, just pushing myself for miles and miles until my feet ache. But yes, to answer your question, there's barely been a week since winter where it hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about my weight? No, I really don't think I would. The whole reason I do it is because of the weight. In fact, what finally made me say something and seek help is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up, and then I just come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. \n\nMy husband never even notices. He’s usually completely engrossed in his history documentaries in the living room. He absolutely loves those World War II specials on PBS. We actually went to Normandy for our 25th anniversary, which was incredibly moving, but the flight over was just a complete nightmare. I lost my reading glasses—my absolute favorite pair of tortoiseshell cat-eyes, beautiful handmade Italian acetate—somewhere in Charles de Gaulle airport. The baggage handlers were on strike, of course, so it was absolute chaos, and I had to navigate the train system without being able to read the small print on the transit maps! They induce so much unwanted base-in prism, it's a miracle I didn't get a blinding migraine right there in front of the Eiffel Tower. You really do get what you pay for when it comes to visual acuity...\n\nOh, dear... I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry, my mind just completely wandered off to Paris. Even though my daughter and this lovely woman from my church—Mrs. Higgins, she sings in the choir—have both told me I look too thin, I still panic if my skirt feels even a little bit snug or if the scale goes up by even a single pound. It's as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight. \n\nIt's funny you mention mirrors, though, because the quality of mirrors these days is just shocking. Most people don't realize that a cheap mirror can actually distort your reflection quite a bit. It has to do with the thickness of the glass and how the silvering is applied to the back. We have these specialized, high-definition mirrors in the optical shop so patients can see exactly how a frame looks on their face, and the clarity is just breathtaking. But you go into a department store dressing room, and the mirrors are usually bowed or warped. It's like a funhouse! I remember trying on a dress at Macy's last spring—a lovely floral wrap dress for my niece's graduation—and the mirror was so poorly mounted that it made my legs look like they were completely different lengths. It's all about the plane of the reflection. If the glass isn't perfectly flat, the light waves scatter, and... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again. Talking about light waves and mirrors. I'm so sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, even without a mirror, I think I'd still be terrified of gaining weight. The panic just... it just happens, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to restrict your food intake if you didn't have the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? I try not to be a snob about my profession, I really do, but Mary showed up wearing these awful drugstore polarized lenses last Tuesday. I told her, \"Mary, you simply cannot buy cheap polarization!\" The film is just glued onto the outside of the lens, and the moment it gets hot or you sweat on it—which, of course, we do while walking—it starts peeling and bubbling at the edges. Because, like I said before, my daughter and Mrs. Higgins from church keep telling me I look too thin, but it just doesn't register that way in my head. If the scale goes up by even a single pound, or if my skirt feels just a tiny bit snug around the waist, my heart just drops. I feel this absolute terror, like I've somehow let myself become huge overnight, and I just have to fix it immediately. \n\nIt's actually a lot like when a patient comes in convinced their prescription is wrong because the new glasses feel \"off.\" Most of the time, it's not the prescription at all, it's the base curve of the lens. The base curve is the front curvature of the lens, and if the lab changes it from what the patient is used to wearing, it can make the world feel like it's swimming or bowing out at the edges. The brain is so used to processing the world through one specific curve that when you change it, it causes this visual panic. We call it \"swim effect.\" I had a gentleman in just last week—lovely man, retired accountant—who was absolutely certain we'd ruined his bifocals, but I just had to explain to him that the index of refraction in the new high-index plastic required a flatter base curve to fit in his semi-rimless frame. You see, the higher the index, the denser the material, which means light bends... \n\nOh, dear. I'm so sorry. I've done it again, haven't I? The base curves and the... right. Yes. The throwing up. It's the fear of gaining weight. Even when people tell me I'm thin, that panic just takes over, and I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as overweight if you couldn't see your reflection in a mirror?\n\nPatient: That's an interesting question... if I couldn't see my reflection. I don't know, honestly. I suppose I might still feel heavy, just from how my clothes fit or how my body feels when I move, but the mirror definitely makes it worse. \n\nIt's funny you mention reflections, though, because dealing with reflections is basically my entire day at the shop. The amount of glare people put up with is just astounding to me. They'll buy these beautiful, expensive frames and then decline the anti-reflective coating to save a few dollars. But what they don't understand is that without AR coating, about eight percent of the light just bounces right off the surface of the lens instead of passing through to the eye. It creates these terrible ghost images, especially when driving at night. You'll see halos around streetlights and headlights. And if you're doing a lot of computer work, the glare from the screen bouncing off the front of your lenses can cause such severe asthenopia—that's eye strain. A good multi-layer AR coating uses destructive interference, which is this incredible principle of physics where the light waves actually cancel each other out, allowing almost 99 percent of the light to transmit through...\n\nOh, good heavens. I am so sorry. I'm rambling about coatings now. I just... I don't know why I keep doing this. But to your question about the mirror, I really don't know for sure. I think I'd probably still feel like I was too big, even without seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior of making yourself throw up after eating would change if you had a more accurate perception of your body shape and weight?\n\nPatient: If I had a more accurate perception... I honestly don't know. It's hard to even imagine what that would look like, to see myself the way other people supposedly do. Would it stop me from making myself sick? Maybe? I'm just really not sure. \n\nIt's a bit like... well, speaking of accurate perception, it is absolutely fascinating how subjective human vision actually is. We fit people for progressive lenses all the time, and their expectation is always this perfect, seamless vision at every distance. But the reality of a progressive lens design is that there is always inherent distortion in the lower periphery—it's mathematically unavoidable when you're blending multiple prescriptions into one piece of plastic or glass. We call it surface astigmatism. The brain literally has to learn to ignore those blurry edges and train the eye to drop down into the central corridor of clear vision. Some patients adapt in a day, their brains just remap the visual field and they see perfectly. But others just cannot handle the peripheral distortion at all; they feel like the floor is tilted or they're walking in a fishbowl. It all comes down to neuroplasticity and how the visual cortex processes the optical data coming through the optic nerve...\n\nOh, Lord. I've gone right off the rails again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. My husband always says I could talk to a brick wall about lens designs. What were we... right, my body shape. Yes. I just... I really don't know if having an accurate perception would change the throwing up. I can't quite picture what seeing myself accurately would even feel like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I remember trying on a dress at Macy's last spring—a lovely floral wrap dress for my niece's graduation—and the mirror was so poorly mounted that it made my legs look like they were completely different lengths. It's all about the plane of the reflection. If the glass isn't perfectly flat, the light waves scatter, and... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again. Talking about light waves and mirrors. I'm so sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, even without a mirror, I think I'd still be terrified of gaining weight. The panic just... it just happens, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to restrict your food intake if you didn't have the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: If I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? Oh, no, absolutely not. The only reason I do those things is because of the weight. Like, after one of those bad evenings, the next day I’ll skip breakfast and lunch or push myself to do extra laps with my walking group, not because I feel energetic, but because I’m trying to undo what I ate. \n\nSpeaking of the walking group, you would not believe the sunglasses some of these women wear out there in the sun. I try not to be a snob about my profession, I really do, but Mary showed up wearing these awful drugstore polarized lenses last Tuesday. I told her, \"Mary, you simply cannot buy cheap polarization!\" The film is just glued onto the outside of the lens, and the moment it gets hot or you sweat on it—which, of course, we do while walking—it starts peeling and bubbling at the edges. It causes terrible distortion. You need a lens where the polarizing filter is actually sandwiched between the layers of polycarbonate or Trivex. Trivex is really the best material for active wear anyway. It was originally developed by the military for helicopter windshields, did you know that? It's incredibly impact-resistant but much lighter than standard plastic, so your frames won't constantly slide down your nose when you perspire...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I've gone completely off track again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. I just can't help myself when it comes to eyewear, I get so passionate about proper UV protection. But yes, to answer your question... the restricting. I only skip those meals and do all that grueling extra walking to make up for the food. If I didn't care about my weight, I wouldn't do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still have the desire to throw up after eating if you didn't have any concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: If I didn't care about how I looked? No, I really wouldn't. The whole reason I... I mean, I've said this, haven't I? The throwing up is entirely because of the weight and how I look. It's the only reason I do it. Like I mentioned earlier, the thing that finally made me realize I needed to talk to someone was the hiding. I'd eat too much, go upstairs to make myself sick, and then come back down pretending I was just getting ready for bed or tidying up the kitchen. \n\nSpeaking of the kitchen, we just had the most gorgeous pendant lights installed over the island. They're this beautiful brushed brass, but the real star is the Edison bulbs we chose. People love the look of those vintage filaments, but they don't realize how much yellow light they cast. It can really strain your eyes if you're trying to read a recipe or chop vegetables. I actually brought home a pair of glasses with a specialized blue-light blocking tint—not the ugly yellow ones you see online, but a very subtle, premium anti-reflective coating that reflects the high-energy visible light away from the eye. It's fascinating how different wavelengths of light interact with the cornea and the lens of the eye. You see, when you're looking at something up close under warm artificial light, your eyes have to work harder to focus because the longer wavelengths... \n\nOh, for pity's sake. I'm doing it again. I'm so sorry. I just... I get talking about optics and I completely lose my train of thought. But yes, to answer your question, if I didn't care about my appearance, I wouldn't be sneaking upstairs to throw up. It's all because of how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior of making yourself throw up after eating is more because of the fear of gaining weight or because you see yourself as overweight even when others say you are not?\n\nPatient: Well, I think... I think it's really the fear. The sheer panic of gaining weight. Because, like I said before, my daughter and Mrs. Higgins from church keep telling me I look too thin, but it just doesn't register that way in my head. If the scale goes up by even a single pound, or if my skirt feels just a tiny bit snug around the waist, my heart just drops. I feel this absolute terror, like I've somehow let myself become huge overnight, and I just have to fix it immediately. \n\nIt's actually a lot like when a patient comes in convinced their prescription is wrong because the new glasses feel \"off.\" Most of the time, it's not the prescription at all, it's the base curve of the lens. The base curve is the front curvature of the lens, and if the lab changes it from what the patient is used to wearing, it can make the world feel like it's swimming or bowing out at the edges. The brain is so used to processing the world through one specific curve that when you change it, it causes this visual panic. We call it \"swim effect.\" I had a gentleman in just last week—lovely man, retired accountant—who was absolutely certain we'd ruined his bifocals, but I just had to explain to him that the index of refraction in the new high-index plastic required a flatter base curve to fit in his semi-rimless frame. You see, the higher the index, the denser the material, which means light bends... \n\nOh, dear. I'm so sorry. I've done it again, haven't I? The base curves and the... right. Yes. The throwing up. It's the fear of gaining weight. Even when people tell me I'm thin, that panic just takes over, and I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as overweight if you couldn't see your reflection in a mirror?\n\nPatient: That's an interesting question... if I couldn't see my reflection. I don't know, honestly. I suppose I might still feel heavy, just from how my clothes fit or how my body feels when I move, but the mirror definitely makes it worse. \n\nIt's funny you mention reflections, though, because dealing with reflections is basically my entire day at the shop. The amount of glare people put up with is just astounding to me. They'll buy these beautiful, expensive frames and then decline the anti-reflective coating to save a few dollars. But what they don't understand is that without AR coating, about eight percent of the light just bounces right off the surface of the lens instead of passing through to the eye. It creates these terrible ghost images, especially when driving at night. You'll see halos around streetlights and headlights. And if you're doing a lot of computer work, the glare from the screen bouncing off the front of your lenses can cause such severe asthenopia—that's eye strain. A good multi-layer AR coating uses destructive interference, which is this incredible principle of physics where the light waves actually cancel each other out, allowing almost 99 percent of the light to transmit through...\n\nOh, good heavens. I am so sorry. I'm rambling about coatings now. I just... I don't know why I keep doing this. But to your question about the mirror, I really don't know for sure. I think I'd probably still feel like I was too big, even without seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior of making yourself throw up after eating would change if you had a more accurate perception of your body shape and weight?\n\nPatient: If I had a more accurate perception... I honestly don't know. It's hard to even imagine what that would look like, to see myself the way other people supposedly do. Would it stop me from making myself sick? Maybe? I'm just really not sure. \n\nIt's a bit like... well, speaking of accurate perception, it is absolutely fascinating how subjective human vision actually is. We fit people for progressive lenses all the time, and their expectation is always this perfect, seamless vision at every distance. But the reality of a progressive lens design is that there is always inherent distortion in the lower periphery—it's mathematically unavoidable when you're blending multiple prescriptions into one piece of plastic or glass. We call it surface astigmatism. The brain literally has to learn to ignore those blurry edges and train the eye to drop down into the central corridor of clear vision. Some patients adapt in a day, their brains just remap the visual field and they see perfectly. But others just cannot handle the peripheral distortion at all; they feel like the floor is tilted or they're walking in a fishbowl. It all comes down to neuroplasticity and how the visual cortex processes the optical data coming through the optic nerve...\n\nOh, Lord. I've gone right off the rails again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry. My husband always says I could talk to a brick wall about lens designs. What were we... right, my body shape. Yes. I just... I really don't know if having an accurate perception would change the throwing up. I can't quite picture what seeing myself accurately would even feel like.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to throw up after eating more related to the fear of gaining weight or because you genuinely believe you are overweight?\n\nPatient: I think we touched on this a bit earlier, but... it really is the fear. It's just this overwhelming panic. Like I was saying, my daughter and my friend from church are always telling me I’m actually too thin. But if I step on the scale and it's up even just one single pound, or if my skirt feels the slightest bit tight around my waist, I just lose it. It's like I’ve somehow blown up and become huge overnight, and I just have to get rid of the food to fix it.\n\nSpeaking of things looking huge, it's actually exactly like what happens with strong plus prescriptions for hyperopia. When someone is very farsighted, their lenses are thicker in the middle, which creates this massive magnification effect. If you don't fit them properly, their eyes look absolutely enormous behind the glasses, like bug eyes. We try to mitigate it by using an aspheric lens design, which flattens the front curve, and we adjust the nose pads to keep the vertex distance—that's the distance from the back of the lens to the cornea—as short as possible. I was fitting Mrs. Gable just on Tuesday, and she has a plus seven prescription, and she was so worried about her eyes looking magnified, so I had to explain how choosing a smaller frame eye size and minimizing the decentration... \n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I'm doing it again. I am so sorry, doctor. You must think I'm completely incapable of staying on topic. But yes, to answer your question, it's the fear. I just get so terrified of gaining weight, no matter what anyone else says about how I look."
